{
  "status": "success",
  "answer": "An analysis of the 143 papers in this notebook reveals several **dominant methodological approaches** that have evolved to address the unique constraints of recommendation systems, search engines, and online marketing platforms. These approaches can be categorized into seven distinct paradigms:\n\n---\n\n### **1. Entire-Space Multi-Task Learning (Addressing SSB and Data Sparsity)**\n*   **Summary:** Post-click conversion rate (CVR) models historically suffered from **Sample Selection Bias (SSB)** because they were trained only on clicked impressions but used to rank all impressions at serving time [1, 2]. They also suffered from extreme **Data Sparsity** since conversion events are incredibly rare [1, 2]. This approach resolves both issues simultaneously by modeling CVR as part of a joint, sequential probability chain (e.g., Click-Through Rate \\\\(p_{CTR}\\\\) and Click-Through & Conversion Rate \\\\(p_{CTCVR}\\\\)) co-trained over the entire space of all impressions, using shared feature representation layers [1, 3, 4].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *Entire Space Multi-Task Model (ESMM)* (Ma et al., 2018) [1, 3, 5-10]\n    *   *Entire Space Multi-Task Modeling via Post-Click Behavior Decomposition (ESM²)* (Wen et al., 2020) [5, 8, 10, 11]\n    *   *Entire Space Counterfactual Multi-Task Model (ESCM²)* (Wang et al., 2022) [4, 5, 12-15]\n    *   *Meituan's Multi-Step Conversion Sequential Modeling (AITM)* (Xi et al., 2021) [7, 8, 16]\n\n---\n\n### **2. Delayed Feedback Modeling & Survival Analysis**\n*   **Summary:** In industrial applications, conversion events often occur after a significant, highly random time delay [17, 18]. Standard streaming pipelines that collect labels immediately upon click mistakenly treat unresolved conversions as \"negative\" labels, injecting severe false-negative bias [6, 19]. This methodology models feedback delay dynamically using **Survival Analysis** (modeling survival and hazard functions over elapsed time), using Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithms or counterfactual importance sampling weights to correct labels for latent \"fake negatives\" [18, 20-22].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *Modeling Delayed Feedback in Display Advertising (DFM)* (Chapelle, 2014) [17, 23-26]\n    *   *A Nonparametric Delayed Feedback Model (NoDeF)* (Yoshikawa & Imai, 2018) [20, 27-29]\n    *   *Entire Space Delayed Feedback Model (ESDF)* (AAAI'21) [6, 18, 30]\n    *   *Asymptotically Unbiased Estimation for Delayed Feedback via Label Correction (DEFUSE)* (WWW'22) [19, 22, 31, 32]\n    *   *Dual Learning Algorithm for Delayed Conversions (DLA-DF)* (SIGIR'20) [21, 33]\n\n---\n\n### **3. Multi-Objective Optimization & Pareto-Efficient Ranking**\n*   **Summary:** Industrial recommenders must balance multiple competing objectives (e.g., maximizing clicks, user dwell time, monetization, and diversity) rather than optimizing a single CTR proxy [34-36]. Instead of manually adjusting heuristic linear utility weights, this approach uses **multi-objective optimization** to dynamically find the optimal trade-offs [37]. Key techniques include stochastic label aggregation, \\\\(\\epsilon\\\\)-constrained optimization, or leveraging actor-critic frameworks to learn and trace the **Pareto Frontier** of non-dominated ranking solutions [38-40].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *A Pareto-Efficient Algorithm for Multiple Objective Optimization* (Lin et al., 2019) [5, 37, 38, 41-44]\n    *   *Multi-Objective Ranking Optimization Using Stochastic Label Aggregation (MORO)* (Carmel et al., 2020) [45-48]\n    *   *Multi-objective Learning to Rank by Model Distillation* (KDD'24) [5, 49]\n    *   *Generative Re-ranking Model for List-level Multi-objective Optimization (SORT-Gen)* (SIGIR'25) [44, 50]\n\n---\n\n### **4. Reinforcement Learning & Off-Policy Correction**\n*   **Summary:** Traditional recommender models are myopic, optimizing only for the user's immediate next click [36, 51]. To optimize for long-term user satisfaction, retention, and dynamic state transitions, these papers formulate the recommendation process as a **Markov Decision Process (MDP)** [52, 53]. Off-policy correction techniques (such as off-policy REINFORCE or off-policy actor-critic) are applied to safely evaluate and optimize the recommendation policies using only historical logs generated by an older, different policy [54-57].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *SlateQ: A Tractable Decomposition for Reinforcement Learning with Recommendation Sets* (Ie et al., 2019) [52, 58-60]\n    *   *Top-K Off-Policy Correction for a REINFORCE Recommender System* (Chen et al., 2019) [61-65]\n    *   *DRN: A Deep Reinforcement Learning Framework for News Recommendation* (Zheng et al., 2018) [66-69]\n    *   *Long-Term Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning (LOPE)* (Spotify, 2024) [70, 71]\n    *   *Multi-Task Fusion via Reinforcement Learning for Long-Term User Satisfaction* (ArXiv'24) [72, 73]\n\n---\n\n### **5. Causal Uplift Modeling & Surrogate Indexing**\n*   **Summary:** Platforms often struggle to measure the true incremental effect of an exposure (e.g., showing a promotion) versus what the user would have done anyway [74]. **Uplift Modeling** addresses this by estimating the Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE) under a potential outcomes framework, often using double machine learning (DML) to remove confounding bias [75, 76]. Furthermore, when long-term outcomes (such as user retention months from now) are too slow to measure, **Surrogate Index** approaches build predictive models to estimate long-term effects using short-term proxies [77, 78].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *The Surrogate Index: Combining Short-Term Proxies to Estimate Long-Term Treatment Effects* (Athey et al., 2019) [77-86]\n    *   *The Dynamically Adjusted Surrogate Index* (Athey et al., 2025) [87-90]\n    *   *Rankability-enhanced Revenue Uplift Modeling Framework* (Xing Tang, KDD'24) [91-93]\n    *   *Multi-channel Uplift Policy Learning (ReAlloc)* (ArXiv'26) [76, 94-97]\n\n---\n\n### **6. Reciprocal Recommendation & Matching Markets**\n*   **Summary:** Unlike traditional recommenders, which only focus on satisfying the preferences of a single target user, **Reciprocal Recommender Systems (RRS)** operate in two-sided matching markets (e.g., job recruitment and online dating) where a match is only successful if *both* parties express mutual interest [98-100]. This methodology integrates bilateral preference probabilities (usually aggregated via a Harmonic Mean) [101] and applies matching theory formulations (such as Gale-Shapley, Transferable Utility (TU) matching, or Nash Social Welfare) to alleviate popularity congestion and balance mutual utility [102-105].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *Fair Reciprocal Recommendation in Matching Markets* (Tomita & Yokoyama, 2024) [100, 102, 106-108]\n    *   *Matching Theory-based Recommender Systems in Online Dating* (Tomita et al., 2022) [108, 109]\n    *   *Revisiting Reciprocal Recommender Systems: Metrics, Formulation, and Method* (Yang et al., 2024) [104, 110-112]\n    *   *Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning for Matching Markets* (RecSys'25) [113-115]\n\n---\n\n### **7. Large-Scale Generative Recommendation (GRMs)**\n*   **Summary:** Rather than maintaining the traditional, isolated multi-stage ranking cascade (Retrieval \\\\(\\rightarrow\\\\) Pre-ranking \\\\(\\rightarrow\\\\) Ranking \\\\(\\rightarrow\\\\) Re-ranking), modern architectures are increasingly moving toward unifying the entire recommendation pipeline into a single **Generative Recommendation** model [116-118]. These models index large-scale catalogs using quantized semantic identifiers and formulate recommendation as an autoregressive sequence transduction task, often aligned via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) [117, 119, 120].\n*   **Representative Papers:**\n    *   *Actions Speak Louder than Words: Trillion-Parameter Sequential Transducers* (Meta AI, ICML'24) [1, 121-123]\n    *   *OneRec: Unifying Retrieve and Rank with Generative Recommender and Preference Alignment* (Kuaishou, 2025) [119, 124, 125]\n    *   *GenRec: An LLM-Backed Recommendation Ranker at Netflix* (ArXiv'26) [120, 125]\n\n***\n\n📊 I can write a Python script in our sandbox using matplotlib to generate a structured timeline mapping out the evolutionary progression of these methodologies—from early heuristic matching to modern entire-space and generative recommender architectures. Would you like me to build this visualization?",

    {
      "source_id": "f9d15f60-f3ba-4949-bab1-c1ce17615a03",
      "citation_number": 3,
      "cited_text": "Modeling over entire space. Eq.(1) gives us hints, which can be transformed into Eq.(2). p(z = 1|y = 1,x) = p(y = 1, z = 1|x) p(y = 1|x) (2) Here p(y = 1, z = 1|x) and p(y = 1|x) are modeled on dataset of S with all impressions. Eq.(2) tells us that with estimation of pCTCVR and pCTR, pCVR can be derived over the entire input space X, which addresses the sample selection bias problem directly. This seems easy by estimating pCTR and pCTCVR with individually trained models separately and obtaining pCVR by Eq.(2), which we refer to asDIVISION for simplicity. However, pCTR is a small number practically, divided by which would arise numerical instability. ESMM avoids this with the multiplication form. In ESMM, pCVR is just an intermediate variable which is constrained by the equation of Eq.(1). pCTR and pCTCVR are the main factors ESMM actually estimated over entire space. The multiplication form enables the three associated and co-trained estimators to exploit the sequential patten of data and communicate information with each other during training. Besides, it ensures the value of estimated pCVR to be in range of [0,1], which in DIVISION method might exceed 1."
    },

      "citation_number": 7,
      "cited_text": "5.3 Baseline Methods We compare the proposed method with the following competitive and mainstream models: LightGBM [9]: LightGBM is a gradient boosting framework that uses tree based learning algorithms. LightGBM is being widely-used in many winning solutions of machine learning competitions4. MLP [4]: We use the base structure of our AITM framework as the single task model. It is a Multi-Layer Perceptron. ESMM [15, 23]: The ESMM and 𝐸𝑆𝑀2 with Probability-Transfer pattern are designed for solving the non-end-to-end post-click conversion rate via training on the entire space to relieve the sample selection bias problem. OMoE [14]: The OMoE with Expert-Bottom pattern integrates experts via sharing one gate among all tasks. MMoE [14]: The MMoE with Expert-Bottom pattern is designed to integrate experts via multiple gates in the Gate Control as shown in Figure 2 (a). PLE [19]: The Progressive Layered Extraction (PLE) with Expert-Bottom pattern separates task-shared experts and task-specific experts explicitly. This is the state-of-the-art method under different task correlations."

      "cited_text": "4https://github.com/microsoft/LightGBM The Probability-Transfer pattern-based ESMM achieves a relatively small improvement due to only simple probability information is transferred between adjacent tasks.       "source_id": "4eb91866-7a34-46eb-98ae-42405788f46c",
      "citation_number": 17,
      "cited_text": "Oracle Same as above, but the labels are given by an oracle that can look into the future to determine if there will be a conversion. This is an upper bound on the best achievable performance as it is oblivious to the delay issue. Shifted The training set is shifted 30 days in the past in order to correctly label all the clicks. There is thus no more uncertainty in the labels, but the dataset is 30 days old. Rescale Same as Naive, but the probabilities of conversion are divided by a constant, which is the overall probability of having a missing label on the test set. This is in fact the method of choice when learning with positive and unlabeled data and the labels are missing at random (see [4, Lemma 1])."

      "source_id": "375b555a-6163-48ad-b6c8-e4e05def5ec2",
      "citation_number": 35,
      "cited_text": "As illustrated in Figure 2 , our final FSM-MMoE-VST architecture combines a Fresh Signal Model for shallow engagement (SMP) with MMoE for joint modeling of deeper engagement, retention, and monetization targets (LMP, chat, follow, spend), integrated with the VST module at inference. Our task grouping is motivated by distinct characteristics of viewer actions: • SMP (shallow engagement): Serves as a critical early indicator requiring immediate prediction, maintained as an independent FSM. • LMP (deep engagement): Represents sustained viewer interest beyond initial exploration. While LMP uses immediate labels rather than delayed windows, its role as a deeper engagement signal aligns naturally with delayed action targets, making it suitable for joint modeling. • Delayed actions (chat, follow, spend): Represent community engagement, retention, and monetization objectives, captured using 14-day delayed windows to address sparsity."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "2efbddee-ea30-49a0-b0af-584812662706",
      "citation_number": 36,
      "cited_text": "CCS Concepts Information systems → Rank aggregation; • Computing methodologies→ Reinforcement learning. ∗These authors contributed equally to this work. Keywords Recommender System, Ranking Utility Tuning, Deep Reinforce-ment Learning, Pareto Frontier Sweeping 1 Introduction Large-scale recommender systems predict multiple objectives simultaneously (e.g., clicks, saves) [13, 36], and a downstream utility layer aggregates the per-objective predictions into a single score used for ranking. A common implementation is a linear utility: a weighted sum of the predicted outcomes, where the weights encode the desired balance among business goals. This design is attractive because the utility layer is simple, debuggable, and can be updated without retraining the ranker. In practice, this makes the utility layer the primary control surface for responding quickly to shifting priorities, while keeping ranker retraining on a different cycle."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "670273a1-bfae-4b62-94a7-dac93de83f9d",
      "citation_number": 37,
      "cited_text": "The contributions of this work are: We propose a general Pareto efcient algorithmic framework (PE-LTR) for multi-objective recommendation. The framework is both model and objective agnostic, which shows its great scalability. We propose a two-step algorithm which theoretically guarantees the Pareto efciency. Despite the algorithm is built upon scalarization technique, it difers from other scalarization approaches with its theoretical guarantee and its automatic learning of scalarization weights rather than manually assignment. With PE-LTR as the cornerstone, we present how to generate the Pareto Frontier and a specifc recommendation. Specifcally, we propose to select a fair recommendation from the Pareto Frontier with proper fairness metrics. We use E-Commerce recommendation as a specifcation of PE-LTR, and conduct extensive online and ofine experiments on a real-world recommender system. The results indicate that our algorithm outperforms other state-of-the-art approaches signifcantly and the solutions generated are Pareto efcient. We open-source a large-scale E-Commerce recommendation dataset EC-REC, which contains the real records of impressions, clicks and purchases. To the best of our knowledge, no public dataset includes all three labels and enough features, this dataset can be used for further studies."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "670273a1-bfae-4b62-94a7-dac93de83f9d",
      "citation_number": 38,
      "cited_text": "cient. The condition is equivalent to a constrained optimization problem, and we propose an algorithm that solves the problem in two steps. First we simplify the problem by relaxing the constraints so that an analytic solution is achieved; then we get the feasible solution by conducting a projection procedure. With PE-LTR as the cornerstone, we provide methods to generate the Pareto Frontier and a specifc recommendation, depending on the needs of service providers. To generate the Pareto Frontier, one can run PE-LTR by evenly set the bounds of the objective scalarization weights. To generate a specifc recommendation, one can either run PE-LTR once with proper bounds or generate the Pareto Frontier frst and choose a \"fair\" solution with specifc fairness metric."
    },

      "cited_text": "Our approach depends to some extent on the nature of the user choice model, but critically on the interaction it has with subsequent user behavior, specifically, how it influences both expected engagement (i.e., reward) and user latent state (i.e., state transition probabilities). We require two assumptions to derive the SLATEQ decomposition. Single choice (SC): A user consumes a single item from each slate (which may be the null item ⊥). Reward/transition dependence on selection (RTDS): The realized reward (user engagement) R(s,A) depends (perhaps stochastically) only on the item i ∈ A consumed by the user (which may also be the null item ⊥). Similarly, the state transition P (s′|s,A) depends only on the consumed i ∈ A."

      "source_id": "259cc507-cd38-4614-87bc-9e8d7aceb867",
      "citation_number": 53,
      "cited_text": "2.2 Policy Learning Reinforcement Learning is a class of learning problems in which the goal of an agent (or multi-agent) to find the policy to optimize some measures of its long-term performance. SIGWEB Newsletter Spring 2019 4 · Xiangyu Zhao et al RL solutions can be categorized in different ways. Here we investigate them from two perspectives: whether the full model is available and the way of finding the optimal policy. 2.2.1 Model-based v.s. Model-free. Reinforcement learning algorithms, which explicitly learn system models and use them to solve MDP problems, are model-based methods. Model-based RL has a strong influence from the control theory and is often explained in terms of different disciplines. These methods include popular algorithms such as the Dyna [Sutton 1991], Prioritized Sweeping [Moore and Atkeson 1993], Q-iteration [Buso-niu et al. 2010], Policy Gradient (PG) [Williams 1992], and the variation of PG [Baxter and Bartlett 2001; Kakade 2001]. The model-free methods ignore the model and just focus on figuring out the value functions directly from the interaction with the environment. To accomplish this, the methods depend on sampling and observation heavily; thus they don’t need to know the inner working of the system. Some examples of these methods are Q-learning [Kröse 1995], SARSA [Rummery and Niranjan 1994], LSPI [Lagoudakis and Parr 2003], and Actor-Critic [Konda and Tsitsiklis 1999]."
    },

      "source_id": "f5a62abd-107f-4425-81cc-c84115c70732",
      "citation_number": 70,
      "cited_text": "The Proposed Method (Long-term Off-policy Evaluation or LOPE) To overcome the limitations of both LCI and OPE methods, we introduce a new method named Long-term Off-Policy Evaluation (LOPE). LOPE is a new OPE problem where we aim to estimate the long-term outcome of a new policy, but we can use short-term rewards and short-term experiment data in addition to historical data. To solve this new statistical estimation problem efficiently, we develop a new estimator based on a decomposition of the expected long-term reward function into surrogate and action effects. The surrogate effect is a component of the long-term reward explained by the observable short-term rewards, while the action effect is the residual term that cannot be captured solely by the short-term surrogates and is also influenced by the specific choice of actions or items. The surrogacy assumption of LCI can be viewed as a special case of this reward function decomposition since surrogacy is an assumption that completely ignores the action effect. Therefore, LOPE is a more general formulation than LCI."
    },

      "source_id": "7fee3f6b-6faa-436e-aa21-d9477fef7739",

    {
      "source_id": "b0d40032-08a0-4c62-ad6b-c138b9a2649d",
      "citation_number": 73,
      "cited_text": "Given a user query, the quality of the ranking results is a leading factor in affecting user satisfaction. Early works [23, 25, 36, 37] usually only consider a single instant metric, e.g., Click-Through Rate (CTR), and train a ranking model over this metric. In practice, it is usually hard to measure real user satisfaction with just one metric. For example, in news recommendation scenario, users may click the recommended news with click bait like the cover pictures or titles but quickly exit when they are not interested in the content. Clearly, only considering CTR will cause a lot of improper recommendations and this encourages us to explore multiple metrics through different user behaviors. When more than one metric is considered, an essential question arises, as to how should these metrics be combined to optimize ranking quality."
    },

      "source_id": "ca3a7f72-3089-435d-a0ba-7bea28e3ec04",
      "citation_number": 75,
      "cited_text": "In recent years, with the development of uplift modeling, there have been many works proposed to solve the above challenge ( Zhang et al. , 2021 ). These works can be divided into three research lines: 1) Meta-learner-based. The basic idea of this line is to estimate the users' responses by using existing predictive models as the base learner. Two of the most representative methods are S-Learner and TLearner (K¨ unzel et al. , 2019 ), which adopt a global base learner and two base learners corresponding to the treatment and control groups, respectively. 2) Tree (or Forest)-based. The basic idea of this line is to employ a hierarchical tree structure to systematically partition the user population into sub-populations that exhibit sensitivity to specific treatments. An essential step involves modeling the uplift directly by applying diverse splitting criteria, including considerations of distribution divergences ( Radcliffe & Surry , 2011 ) and expected responses ( Zhao et al. , 2017 ; Saito et al. , 2020 ). 3) Neural network-based. The basic idea of this line is to leverage the power of neural networks to develop estimators that are both intricate and versatile in predicting the user's response. CFRNet ( Shalit et al. , 2017 ) uses the Integer Probabilistic Metric (IPM) to solve the selection bias"

      "citation_number": 77,
      "cited_text": "Rather than rely on single short-term outcomes to proxy for long-term ones, surrogate index approaches [Athey et al., 2019] have become a popular way to harness rich observations to address this issue. This type of approach first builds a model to predict the long-term outcome of interest from a set of short-term outcomes. If this set of short-term outcomes taken together satisfies the “surrogacy assumption” [Prentice, 1989], namely that they fully mediate all of the treatment’s long-term effects and that their effect on the long term can be identified, then long-term treatment effects can be properly estimated using the predicted long-term outcome instead."

      "source_id": "3933c4bd-83a3-4413-91d5-fa749fabcafa",
      "citation_number": 82,
      "cited_text": "3.1 Identification via the Surrogate Index Athey et al. (2025b) rely on three key assumptions to identify the long-term ATE. First, the standard unconfoundedness assumption in the experimental sample (A⊥⊥(Y (a), S(a)) | X,G = E) is assumed to hold. Second, the surrogacy assumption (A⊥⊥Y | S,X,G = E), originally proposed by Prentice (1989), posits that the treatment A provides no additional information about the primary outcome Y beyond what is already contained in the surrogates S and pre-treatment variables X in the experimental sample. Third, the comparability assumption requires that the conditional outcome distribution is stable across samples, i.e., G ⊥ Y | S,X."
    },

      "cited_text": "Baselines. (2) Additive ROI models channel responses independently, reflecting the decoupled paradigm in industry.       "citation_number": 98,
      "cited_text": "tend to judge options according to multiple criteria, in a multi-criteria RS a user may often prefer to rate items (e.g. hotels) using several criteria (e.g. food, cleanliness, service), hence these systems were proposed to exploit such ratings [100]. An emerging family of RS approaches arose in the last decade, Reciprocal RS (RRS) [109, 116], in which: (i) users become the item being recommended to other users, and (ii) success is determined not only by the end user who requested recommendations, but also by the user(s) being recommended,"
    },
    {
      "source_id": "5f2155d9-f8d0-4247-a21e-eef7f102c721",
      "citation_number": 99,
      "cited_text": "Naive and Reciprocal methods. Moreover, TU method outperforms SW with themisspecified examination function inmost cases, and it also works well even in relatively large datasets where SW method does not work. 1.1 Related work Reciprocal recommendation. Of particular distinction in reciprocal recommendation from the standard item recommendation is that a system must consider the bilateral preferences of proactive and reactive users. Most of the conventional methods estimate the preferences of each side of users by using a corresponding model and then use the bilateral preference estimates to predict a reciprocal preference. As the first step of unilateral preferences can be solved by standard recommender methods, modeling reciprocal preferences is often reduced into the aggregation of bilateral preferences. In this context, various aggregation functions have been proposed, e.g., arithmetic mean [33, 34], geometric mean [33, 34], harmonic mean [35, 52], cross-ratio uniform [4], matrix multiplication [22], the multiplicative inverse of rank multiplication [32], and weighted mean with optimized weighting parameters [27]. Neve and Palomares [34] proposed an early method that optimizes two independent matrix factorization models of unilateral preferences and then aggregates two preference estimates using arithmetic, geometric, or harmonic means. However, such aggregation functions highly depend on the scale and domain of unilateral preference estimates and lack a theoretical foundation. By contrast, recent fairness-aware recommendation do not consider such aggregation functions by directly maximizing a welfare function [14, 15]."

      "source_id": "0e509aae-d04d-49d7-bfff-1f6322a88e31",
      "citation_number": 104,
      "cited_text": "In this paper, we systemically revisit the task of reciprocal recommendation, by introducing the new metrics, formulation, and method. Firstly, we propose five new evaluation metrics that comprehensively and accurately assess the performance of RRS from three distinct perspectives: overall coverage, bilateral stability, and balanced ranking. These metrics provide a more holistic understanding of the system's effectiveness and enable a comprehensive evaluation. Furthermore, we formulate the RRS from a causal perspective, formulating recommendations as bilateral interventions, which can better model the decoupled effects of potential influencing factors. By utilizing the potential outcome framework, we further develop a model-agnostic causal reciprocal recommendation method that considers the causal effects of recommendations. Additionally, we introduce a reranking strategy to maximize matching outcomes, as measured by the proposed metrics. Extensive experiments on two real-world datasets from recruitment and dating scenarios demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed metrics and approach. The code and dataset are available at: this https URL ."

      "cited_text": "[2408.09748] Revisiting Reciprocal Recommender Systems: Metrics, Formulation, and Method Skip to main content Search Submit Donate Log in Search arXiv Press Enter to search · Advanced search Computer Science > Information Retrieval arXiv:2408.09748 (cs) [Submitted on 19 Aug 2024] Title: Revisiting Reciprocal Recommender Systems: Metrics, Formulation, and Method Authors: Chen Yang , Sunhao Dai , Yupeng Hou , Wayne Xin Zhao , Jun Xu , Yang Song , Hengshu Zhu View a PDF of the paper titled Revisiting Reciprocal Recommender Systems: Metrics, Formulation, and Method, by Chen Yang and 6 other authors"
    },
    {
      "source_id": "0e509aae-d04d-49d7-bfff-1f6322a88e31",
      "citation_number": 111,
      "cited_text": "View PDF HTML (experimental) Abstract: Reciprocal recommender systems~(RRS), conducting bilateral recommendations between two involved parties, have gained increasing attention for enhancing matching efficiency. However, the majority of existing methods in the literature still reuse conventional ranking metrics to separately assess the performance on each side of the recommendation process. These methods overlook the fact that the ranking outcomes of both sides collectively influence the effectiveness of the RRS, neglecting the necessity of a more holistic evaluation and a capable systemic solution."
    },

    {
      "source_id": "3e16b474-a652-4380-a68b-1b5664cdad41",
      "citation_number": 116,
      "cited_text": "1 Introduction To balance efficiency and effectiveness, most modern recommender systems adopt a cascade ranking strategy[6, 26, 34, 43]. As illustrated in Figure 1(b), a typical cascade ranking system employs a three-stage pipeline: recall [6, 19, 54], pre-ranking [28, 46], and ranking [2, 3, 15, 16, 33, 52, 53]. Each stage is responsible for selecting the top-𝑘 items from the received items and passing the results to the next stage, collectively balancing the trade-off between system response time and sorting accuracy."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "3e16b474-a652-4380-a68b-1b5664cdad41",
      "citation_number": 117,
      "cited_text": "Although efficient in practice, existing methods typically treat each ranker independently, where the effectiveness of each isolated stage serves as the upper bound for the subsequent ranking stage, thereby limiting the performance of the overall ranking system. Despite various efforts [11, 13, 18, 20, 34, 44] to improve overall recommendation performance by enabling interaction among rankers, they still maintain the traditional cascade ranking paradigm. Re-cently, generative retrieval-based recommendation systems (GRs) [36, 45, 51] have emerged as a promising paradigm by directly generating the identifier of a candidate item in an autoregressive sequence generation manner. By indexing items with quantized semantic IDs that encode item semantics [24], recommenders can leverage the abundant semantic information within the items. The generative nature of GRs makes them suitable for directly selecting candidate items through beam search decoding and producing more diverse recommendation results. However, current generative models only act as selectors in the retrieval stage, as their recommendation accuracy does not yet match that of well-designed multiple cascade rankers."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "2856572a-f50a-4c07-8485-d9b948ea9547",
      "citation_number": 118,
      "cited_text": "In recent years, more complex recommendation paradigm [1, 19] became popular in different industries. This type of multi-stage recommendation system consists of different components including retrieval, ranking, re-ranking and blending. Each components have different responsibility and advantages. For instance, retrieval models are usually efficient and can filter billions of items to thousands of top relevant candidates, while re-ranking and blending algorithms can help fulfill different business objectives such as adding diversity on topics etc. With the rise of Large Language Models [34], large scale generative ranking algorithms [25, 26, 40, 51, 59] have sought to unify multiple components of the ranking pipeline within a single, billion-parameter model trained on web-scale data. While these methods have shown promising online results, these approaches are computational heavy and lack the flexibility required for deployment in different specific use cases such as home page recommendations or related items recommendations. Moreover, optimizing user retention remains challenging in the end-to-end generative ranking framework."

      "cited_text": "LLM-backed ranking that is competitive with a mature production system.Wepresent GenRec, an LLM-backed recommendation ranker built on top of an in-house foundation LLM, and compare it to a long-running, highly engineered production ranker. A GenRec model trained with substantially fewer Phase-2 training examples and input signals achieves statistically significant gains on our primary offline and online metrics. A two-phase, LLM-centric training framework with quantified benefits. We validate a two-phase training scheme in which Phase 1 adapts an open-source LLM to Netflix data to build a Netflix-aware foundation model, and Phase 2 performs high-cadence post-training for recommendation ranking. We articulate the distinct roles and objectives of the two phases and empirically decompose their impact on ranking quality, showing substantial gains from both phases.   "answer": "Across the papers in this notebook, several prominent evaluation datasets and benchmarks are widely shared to validate models targeting delayed feedback, entire-space multi-task learning, reinforcement learning, uplift modeling, and reciprocal recommendations.\n\nThe most common datasets, their characteristics, and the specific papers that employ them are detailed below:\n\n---\n\n### **1. Criteo Datasets (Conversion Logs & Uplift Trials)**\n\n*   **Criteo Conversion Logs (Delayed Feedback)**\n    *   **Description:** Sourced from Criteo's live online display advertising traffic over a period of 60 days, this classical public dataset includes timestamps for clicks and eventual conversions, as well as categorical and continuous features [1-7]. It is the standard benchmark for evaluating **delayed feedback modeling** under continuous or streaming learning protocols [1, 2, 8-10].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Modeling Delayed Feedback in Display Advertising* (DFM) [1]\n        *   *A Nonparametric Delayed Feedback Model* (NoDeF) [11]\n        *   *Addressing Delayed Feedback in CTR Prediction* (Twitter) [2]\n        *   *A Feedback Shift Correction under Delayed Feedback* (FSIW) [12]\n        *   *Delayed Feedback Modeling for Entire Space CVR* (ESDF) [13]\n        *   *Asymptotically Unbiased Estimation via Label Correction* (DEFUSE - evaluated on *Criteo-30d* and *Criteo-1d*) [4]\n        *   *Counterfactual Reward Modification for Streaming Recommendation* [3]\n        *   *Capturing Delayed Feedback via Elapsed-Time Sampling* (ES-DFM) [5]\n        *   *Generalized Delayed Feedback Model* (GDFM) [14]\n        *   *Delayed Feedback Modeling with Influence Functions* (IF-DFM) [15]\n        *   *Online Conversion Rate Prediction via Multi-Interval Screening* (MISS) [9]\n*   **Criteo Uplift Dataset (v2)**\n    *   **Description:** Sourced from Criteo AI Labs, this contains approximately 13.9 million observations from an online advertising incrementality trial [16, 17]. Each row registers ad exposure (treatment) vs. no exposure (control), 11-12 anonymized features, and binary outcomes (*visit* and *conversion*) [16, 18]. It is used extensively for **individual treatment effect (ITE) and causal uplift modeling** [16, 18, 19].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Evaluating Decision Rules Across Many Weak Experiments* [20]\n        *   *PROXIMA: Proxy Metric Validation* [16]\n        *   *Rethinking Causal Ranking Evaluation* [18]\n        *   *Learning the Covariance of Treatment Effects* [21]\n        *   *Evaluating Learning-to-Rank Techniques for Uplift Modeling* [22, 23]\n        *   *Uplift Evaluation on Balanced Groups* [24, 25]\n        *   *Invariant Deep Uplift Modeling for Incentive Assignment* [26]\n\n---\n\n### **2. Taobao & Alibaba Datasets (Ali-CCP & Taobao User Behavior)**\n\n*   **Taobao User Behavior Dataset (Click & Conversion Logs)**\n    *   **Description:** Collected from real-world daily click and conversion logs in Alibaba's Taobao recommender system [4, 5, 27]. These logs contain massive streams of sequential user actions—such as impressions, clicks, adding items to carts or favorites, and buying/conversions [14, 27, 28]. They are used to validate **entire-space multi-task modeling** and **delayed feedback correction** [13, 27-29].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Entire Space Multi-Task Model* (ESMM) [27, 30]\n        *   *Entire Space Behavior Decomposition* (ESM²) [28]\n        *   *Entire Space Delayed Feedback Model* (ESDF) [13, 31]\n        *   *Asymptotically Unbiased Estimation* (DEFUSE) [4, 32]\n        *   *Capturing Delayed Feedback via Elapsed-Time Sampling* (ES-DFM) [5]\n        *   *Generalized Delayed Feedback Model* (GDFM) [14]\n        *   *Delayed Feedback Modeling with Influence Functions* (IF-DFM) [15]\n*   **Ali-CCP (Alibaba Click and Conversion Prediction)**\n    *   **Description:** An off-shelf public dataset from Alibaba containing around 84 million impressions, clicks, purchases, and sparse categorical user/item features [33, 34].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Large-Scale Causal Approaches to Debiasing CVR* [33, 35]\n        *   *Modeling Sequential Dependence among Audience Conversions* (AITM) [34, 36]\n        *   *Entire Space Counterfactual Multi-Task Model* (ESCM²-IPS & ESCM²-DR) [37]\n\n---\n\n### **3. MovieLens (ML-1M, ML-20M) & Netflix Datasets**\n\n*   **MovieLens Datasets**\n    *   **Description:** Highly popular collaborative filtering benchmarks containing anonymous ratings of movies [38, 39]. They are frequently repurposed as sequential, interactive, or episodic environments to test **user retention optimization** and **off-policy reinforcement learning** (by converting rating thresholds into binary labels, e.g., ratings \\\\(\\ge 4\\\\) treated as a satisfied click/match) [38, 40, 41].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Neural Interactive Collaborative Filtering* (NICF) [40]\n        *   *Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive User Retention Optimization* (AURO) [38]\n        *   *Maximum-Entropy Regularized Decision Transformer* (EDT4Rec) [39]\n        *   *Sequential Recommendation for Optimizing Long-Term Retention* [41, 42]\n        *   *Future Impact Decomposition in Request-Level Recommendations* [43]\n        *   *Actions Speak Louder than Words* (Meta's HSTU evaluation) [44, 45]\n*   **Netflix Prize Dataset**\n    *   **Description:** Comprising over 100 million user movie ratings, it is utilized as an offline benchmark for learning user preferences and tracking interest drift over time [39, 40, 46].\n    *   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Neural Interactive Collaborative Filtering* (NICF) [40]\n        *   *Maximum-Entropy Regularized Decision Transformer* (EDT4Rec) [39]\n\n---\n\n### **4. Hillstrom Dataset (MineThatData)**\n\n*   **Description:** Sourced from Kevin Hillstrom's e-mail analytics challenge, this retail dataset contains 64,000 observations of users targeted with female merchandise emails, male merchandise emails, or no emails (control) [22, 24, 25]. The recorded outcomes include site visits, purchases, and conversion amounts [22, 24]. It serves as a classic benchmark for **uplift modeling** [24, 47].\n*   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *Evaluating Learning-to-Rank Techniques for Uplift Modeling* [22]\n        *   *Uplift Evaluation on Balanced Groups* [24]\n        *   *Rankability-Enhanced Revenue Uplift Modeling* (RERUM) [47]\n\n---\n\n### **5. KuaiRand & KuaiRec Datasets**\n\n*   **Description:** Open-source datasets provided by Kuaishou's short-video platform [19, 48]. They feature rich, multi-modal user interaction sequences (such as watch time/duration, clicks, comments, and forwards) under unbiased exposure conditions (such as randomly exposed video logs) [17, 41, 48].\n*   **Which Papers Use It:**\n        *   *PROXIMA: Proxy Metric Validation* [17, 49]\n        *   *Future Impact Decomposition in Request-Level Recommendations* [43, 50]\n        *   *Sequential Recommendation for Optimizing Long-Term Retention* [41]\n        *   *Maximum-Entropy Regularized Decision Transformer* (EDT4Rec) [39]\n\n---\n\n### **6. Specialized & Professional Reciprocal Datasets**\n\n*   **Tencent Advertising Algorithm Competition Dataset (2017 & 2025)**\n    *   **Description:** Advertising logs spanning 9 to 10 days, capturing clicks, coupon acquisitions, and conversions under delayed signal windows [6, 9, 51, 52].\n    *   *Used by:* *Counterfactual Reward Modification* [51], *Online CVR Prediction via Multi-Interval Screening* [9], and *All-Modality Generative Recommendation Baseline* [52].\n*   **DBLP-CoAuthor & Epinions-Trust**\n    *   **Description:** Graph-based social and citation networks used to formulate professional co-authorship matches and explicit trust declarations [53].\n    *   *Used by:* *Causally Formulated Reciprocal Recommendation* (CFRR) [53].\n*   **Jobs (LaLonde Dataset)**\n    *   **Description:** Composed of randomized and observational data relating to the effect of job training programs on post-training income and employment status [24, 25].\n    *   *Used by:* *Uplift Evaluation on Balanced Groups* [24].\n*   **Wantedly Visit Scouting Logs**\n    *   **Description:** Online A/B test logs collected from a Japanese recruiter-seeker matching platform, used to evaluate off-policy matching estimators [54, 55].\n    *   *Used by:* *Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning for Matching Markets* [54].\n\n***\n\n🎧 Would you like to create an Audio Overview detailing how these industrial platforms (such as Alibaba, Tencent, Spotify, and Kuaishou) leverage these specific benchmarks to solve their real-world machine learning bottlenecks?",

      "cited_text": "5.1 Datasets We evaluate our experiment on both public and industrial datasets. The statistics of the processed datasets are shown in Table 4. In particular, since 30 days attribution period is unbearable for industrial online advertising, we further derive a one-day attribution version, namely Criteo-1d, which uses samples that convert within one day as positive.     {
      "source_id": "adf6d4a9-2fef-4bed-a95e-da11d5b00a5e",
      "citation_number": 8,
      "cited_text": "1The code for reproducing our results on public dataset is available at https://github.com/ThyrixYang/es dfm 2https://labs.criteo.com/2013/12/conversion-logs-dataset/ Streaming Experimental Protocol We have designed an experimental evaluation method for the streaming CVR prediction, which can fully verify the performance of different methods in the online learning settings. In this work, we divide the streaming dataset into multiple datasets according to the click timestamp, each of which contains one hour data. Following the online training manner of industrial systems, the model is trained on the t-th hour data and tested on the t + 1-th hour data, then trained on the t+1-th hour data and tested on the t+2-th hour data, and so on and so forth. Note that, the training data is reconstructed with fake negatives, while evaluation data is the original data. Therefore, we report the weighted metrics of the evaluation dataset of different hours to verify the overall performance of different methods on streaming data."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "8b7867b6-068b-4ebe-8f51-91961aead54f",
      "citation_number": 9,
      "cited_text": "Datasets The statistics of the two datasets are given in Table 1. We follow the original maximal attribution window setting of datasets in experiments. Criteo Conversion Logs Criteo1 is a widely used dataset for CVR prediction task (Chen et al. 2022). It contains 60 days data, with a 30 days attribution window 𝑑𝑚𝑎𝑥 . Tencent Advertising Algorithm Competition 2017 Ten-cent dataset2 includes 9 days of data with a 5 days attribution window 𝑑𝑚𝑎𝑥 . The dataset contains 22 million samples. Experimental Settings Online Simulation The online streaming process requires models to keep predicting CVR of new samples and then training on them. Following previous work (Chen et al. 2022; Yang et al. 2021), we separate the dataset into pretraining part and streaming part. Methods are allowed to use all the data from the former part to complete pretraining as they need. Then, models keep getting evaluated and updated hour by hour on the streaming part. The online training data only contains information available at the current timestamps. We adopted three distinctive evaluation metrics to evaluate the model performance, the area under the ROC curve (AUC), the negative log likelihood (NLL), and the precision-recall curve (PR-AUC)."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "63a92168-1e3a-4d41-9dcf-f5495de9dde7",

      "cited_text": "Oracle 0.8429 0.6441 0.3891 100.00% 100.00% 100.00% 0.6537 0.6540 0.6436 100.00% 100.00% 100.00% period of 7 days. The dataset has 6 groups of user features and 5 groups of item features. Data StreamWe divide all datasets into two parts to simulate the continuous training paradigm. The first part of data is used for pre-training the model to obtain a good initialization for continuous training. Note that in case of label leaking, we set the labels as 0 if the conversion occurs in the second part of the data. The models are then trained and evaluated on the second part. According to the click timestamp in the datasets, we divide the second part of data into multiple pieces, each of which contains data of one hour. Following the online trainingmanner of industrial systems, we train models on the t-th hour data and test them on the t + 1-th hour. The reported metrics are the weighted average across different hours on streaming data."

      "cited_text": "For this work, we collect traffic logs from Taobao’s recommender system. The full dataset consists of 8.9 billions samples with sequential labels of click and conversion. Careful experiments are conducted. ESMM consistently outperforms competitive models, which demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. We also release our dataset1 for future research in this area. 2 THE PROPOSED APPROACH 2.1 Notation We assume the observed dataset to be S = {(xi ,yi → zi )}| N i=1, with sample (x ,y → z) drawn from a distribution D with domain X×Y×Z, whereX is feature space,Y andZ are label spaces, and N is the total number of impressions. x represents feature vector of observed impression, which is usually a high dimensional sparse vector with multi-fields, such as user field, item field etc. y and z are binary labels with y = 1 or z = 1 indicating whether click or conversion event occurs respectively. y → z reveals the sequential dependence of click and conversion labels that there is always a preceding click when conversion event occurs."

  "status": "success",
  "answer": "Across the provided papers, several key open problems, research gaps, and theoretical limitations are identified most frequently. They can be systematically grouped into the following distinct categories:\n\n### **1. The Evaluation Gap: Offline Evaluation vs. Live Online Testing**\n*   **Predominance of Offline Evaluations:** Multiple papers note that the vast majority of research in multi-objective optimization, long-term user retention, and reciprocal recommendation is validated strictly through offline simulations or one-shot evaluations using static historical datasets [1-3].\n*   **Lack of Real-World A/B Experiments:** There is a severe shortage of live online A/B testing on real platforms to validate these complex models [1, 4, 5], primarily because long-term online experiments are highly risky and slow down product iteration cycles [6, 7].\n*   **Absence of Tailored Off-Policy Evaluation (OPE):** Traditional OPE methods (like Inverse Propensity Scoring) perform poorly in matching markets due to extremely sparse rewards and the complex, bilateral nature of user-to-user interactions [8-10]. Developing robust OPE and off-policy learning (OPL) methods specifically for reciprocal and two-sided environments remains an open problem [4, 10].\n\n### **2. Long-Term Value (LTV) Attribution and Surrogacy Challenges**\n*   **The Attribution Dilemma:** Directly optimizing for user retention and revisitation is incredibly difficult because return signals are sparse, noisy, and occur over long or indefinite timelines (days, weeks, or months) [11-13]. Attributing a delayed return event to a single, specific recommendation or exposure among thousands of past interactions remains a major scientific challenge [11, 12, 14, 15].\n*   **Unverifiable Surrogacy Assumptions:** Platforms frequently use short-term \"surrogates\" (like clicks or immediate engagement) to estimate long-term outcomes [7, 16, 17]. However, papers flag that standard surrogacy assumptions are often debatable, restrictive, and mathematically unverifiable, leading to severe estimation bias when they are violated [16, 18].\n*   **The Tensions of User Heterogeneity:** Surrogate metrics or fixed reward thresholds (such as defining \"long-dwell\" as a static duration) fail to adapt to diverse user activity levels, leading to unintended cross-surface cannibalization, negative retention trends for non-core users, and an unresolved trade-off between simplicity and accuracy [19-21].\n\n### **3. Strategic Behavior and Game-Theoretic Vulnerability**\n*   **Absence of Strategy-Proofness:** Reciprocal and matching-market recommenders (e.g., online dating, job search) do not operate in a vacuum; users respond to recommendations strategically [22]. \n*   **False Preference Manipulation:** For instance, a user might avoid applying to or liking a highly popular candidate because they anticipate high competitiveness, meaning the raw interaction histories collected by the platform do not reflect true underlying user preferences [22]. Designing \"strategy-proof\" reciprocal recommenders that incentivize users to act according to their true preferences is noted as an important, understudied direction [22].\n\n### **4. Temporal Information Inaccessibility in Upstream Decisions**\n*   **The Inaccessibility Boundary:** In growth and user acquisition scenarios (like real-time bidding for ad campaigns), models must predict a user's long-term retention at the bidding stage *before* the user actually enters the platform [23].\n*   **Bridging Growth and Recommendation Systems:** Although in-app content consumption (onboarding content) heavily dictates whether a user stays, this future signal is strictly inaccessible at decision time [23, 24]. Resolving how to let downstream content consumption patterns inversely guide upstream acquisition decisions without causing severe feature leakage remains a critical open problem [23, 25].\n\n### **5. Modeling Dynamic, Bilateral Preferences**\n*   **Static vs. Sequential Modeling:** The vast majority of reciprocal recommendation systems rely on static user profile matching or static collaborative filtering, neglecting the fact that user tastes dynamically evolve over time [26, 27].\n*   **Dual-Role Representation Complexity:** Unlike standard item recommenders where items are static, users in reciprocal systems serve as both active \"proposers\" and passive \"receivers\" [28, 29]. When one user's preferences shift, they instantly alter other users' representations, making real-time, sequential, and session-based reciprocal modeling exceptionally complex to train and scale [29].\n\n### **6. Scaling Bottlenecks and Continuous Learning Issues**\n*   **Quadratic Computational Complexity:** Optimization and matching-theory-based algorithms (such as the Gale-Shapley or Social Welfare models) often struggle to scale to industrial dimensions (hundreds of thousands of users) due to the high computational complexity of handling massive doubly stochastic matrices or virtual transfers per timestep [4, 30].\n*   **Gradient Dependency and Streaming Instability:** In continuous, real-time training pipelines, models suffer from streaming instabilities, including \"catastrophic forgetting\" of long-term interests as models continuously fit newer, popular incoming samples [31-33], and the temporal dependency of gradients where parameters are updated on fake negative samples before positive ones arrive [31].\n\n### **7. Over-representation of Highly Active Users**\n*   **Activity Bias in Training Labels:** Models aimed at optimizing long-term retention or revisitation are severely biased by data imbalance [34]. Highly active users disproportionately dominate log databases (e.g., by contributing a massive volume of clicks, saves, or repins), which leaves models with too little label data to learn the retention dynamics of hard-to-retain, inactive, or new users [34-36].\n\n***\n\n🎨 I can use Python in our sandbox environment to generate a clean visual concept mapping out the sequential \"Inaccessibility Boundary\" described by Kuaishou's growth engineers (tracking how bidding, ad delivery, conversion, onboarding content consumption, and eventual retention interact over time). Would you like to build this schematic?",

    {
      "source_id": "1c81f0d0-b780-4875-a367-0717630d8421",
      "citation_number": 1,
      "cited_text": "Moreover, as mentioned, in many cases, the objectives both within a category and across categories can be competing and represent a trade-off. Dealing with such trade-offs is a common target in academic literature, in which most evaluations are done offline, i.e., based on historical data and without users in the loop. In such settings, the goal is then to find a balance between two or more computational metrics, e.g., diversity and accuracy. Limited research unfortunately exists that examines potential trade-offs through real-world experiments. The simulation study in Mehrotra et al. ( 2018 ) is an example of a work that is based on real-world A/B test log data, which indicates that increasing the system's fairness may lead to higher user satisfaction and engagement in practice. Also, when considering short-term and long-term objectives, taking measures to increase interactivity and engagement with the system in the short term is sometimes considered beneficial for customer retention in the long run (Gomez-Uribe and Hunt, 2015 )."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "1c81f0d0-b780-4875-a367-0717630d8421",
      "citation_number": 2,
      "cited_text": "Most academic research is based on one-shot evaluations, typically focusing on prediction accuracy given a static dataset and a certain point in time. The longitudinal effects of different recommendation strategies are much less explored and there is also limited literature on the long-term effects of recommender systems in the industry. A/B tests in the industry may last from a few weeks to several months. In Gomez-Uribe and Hunt ( 2015 ), the case of Netflix is discussed, where one main KPI is customer retention, which is oriented toward the long-term perspective. In their case, attributing changes in the recommender system to such long-term effects is reported to be challenging, e.g., because of already high retention rates and the need for large user samples. Other reports from real-world deployments of recommender systems can be found in Panniello et al. ( 2016 ) or Lee and Hosanagar ( 2019 ). In Lee and Hosanagar ( 2019 ), the authors for example found that using a recommender system led to decreased sales diversity compared to a situation without a recommender."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "e3edec23-9654-4bd7-a26c-99f06b789464",

This trend is also observed in evaluations made between with CB-RRS and CF-RRS, as shown throughout the analysis in Section 4. In spite of this, RRS research as of its current state still shows a predominance of CB models,"

      "cited_text": "There are several considerable future directions in the field of reciprocal recommendation in matching markets. Moreover, it is also important to develop offline/off-policy evaluationmethods in reciprocal recommendation in matching markets. Next, more scalable ranking methods in RRSs would also be a future direction. Although our TU method is more efficient in terms of time and space complexity than the Su et al. [49]’s method, it still costs the order of computational complexity 𝑂 ( |J ||C|) for each timestep in Algorithm. 2, and we only conduct experiments with 1,000 × 1,000 datasets at most. Since there are real-world matching platforms with tens or hundreds of thousands of users, a more efficient implementation of our TU method or other algorithms would be needed for such large-scale matching platforms."

      "cited_text": "platforms. For future work, developing theoretically rigorous exact or approximation algorithms for NSW maximization would be an interesting direction. Additionally, adapting alternative fairness frameworks—such as Walrasian equilibrium criteria [60] and Lorenz efficiency [16]—to our model with explicit mutual interest requirements could provide comple- mentary approaches to fair reciprocal recommendation. Finally, online evaluation methodologies such as A/B testing would provide valuable insights into the real-world performance of our methods."

    {
      "source_id": "f5a62abd-107f-4425-81cc-c84115c70732",
      "citation_number": 6,
      "cited_text": "Estimating long-term outcomes Often, we would like to know the long-term consequences, such as a specific metric of interest several months after applying a new algorithm, of algorithmic changes. Intuitively, the most accurate way of doing this is to run an online experiment, or A/B test, of the new algorithm for a sufficiently long period and measure its effectiveness. However, this procedure of running a new algorithm for a long time has several clear downsides: Decision-making of algorithm selection would become extremely slow. For example, if we run a year-long online experiment, we never know what algorithm would work best for a full one year. Running an experiment to test a new algorithm for a long time can be risky, since the new algorithm might be detrimental to the users' experience."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "7fee3f6b-6faa-436e-aa21-d9477fef7739",
      "citation_number": 7,
      "cited_text": "outcome is to run an online experiment or A/B test for the potential algorithms, but it takes months or even longer to observe the long- term outcomes of interest, making the algorithm selection process unacceptably slow. This work thus studies the problem of feasibly yet accurately estimating the long-term outcome of an algorithm using only historical and short-term experiment data. Existing ap- proaches to this problem either need a restrictive assumption about the short-term outcomes called surrogacy or cannot effectively use"
    },

Although these conventional estimation strategies perform well when large amounts of logged data and dense reward observations are available, OPE in matching recommendation settings signifi- cantly deviates from these ideal conditions.     {
      "source_id": "ab9db06f-530d-4123-adce-92e44aa285ad",
      "citation_number": 11,
      "cited_text": "previously accessed and saved. This behavior often signals user satisfaction, typically driven by personalized content and recommendations. Therefore, identifying and leveraging previously saved content that encourages users to return can serve as a valuable signal for fostering long-term user retention. Modeling and optimizing revisitation behavior presents several challenges. The first is attribution: it is difficult to determine what exactly triggers a user to revisit. Many confounding factors can influence revisitation, such as content quality, user experience, and platform notifications or reminders (Adar, Teevan, and Dumais 2008; Zhang et al. 2021). For instance, if a user returns with a completely different intent than in their previous session, it would be inappropriate to attribute that revisit to their earlier activities. The second challenge concerns scale. Since revisitations can occur within a day, a week, or even a month (Adar, Teevan, and Dumais 2009), tracking them effectively requires maintaining extensive records of user activity over indefinite periods. This creates significant data processing and scaling challenges (Jin et al. 2017). To date, there has been little concrete research on whether it is reasonable to disregard revisitations after a certain period (e.g., after X days)."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "2856572a-f50a-4c07-8485-d9b948ea9547",
      "citation_number": 12,
      "cited_text": "Direct optimization of long-term retention is difficult for several reasons. First of all, retention labels are sparse, noisy, and often only weakly observable [43]. Secondly, retention is delayed: even when downstream outcomes are observed, attributing them to specific earlier recommendations is challenging [5, 48]. Finally, user interests and data distributions evolve over time, so fixed surrogate objectives can become stale and lose alignment with the behaviors that actually drive long-term value [50]."
    },

      "source_id": "2856572a-f50a-4c07-8485-d9b948ea9547",
      "citation_number": 13,
      "cited_text": "Definition 2.3 (Proxy User Retention Objective). Similar to Def-inition 2.1, the proxy user retention objective based on downstream rewards can be defined as: Ĵ𝑢 (𝜃 ; 𝛽, 𝜙) = E𝜏∼(𝜋𝜃 ,𝑃𝑢 ) [ 𝑇∑︁ 𝑡=0 𝛽𝑡 R̂𝑢,𝑡 (H𝑢,𝑡 , 𝑥𝑢,𝑡 ) ] 3 Insights and Analysis Directly optimizing long-term retention is difficult because revisit labels are sparse, delayed, and only partially attributable to any recommendation. We therefore use an offline screening framework to identify session-level behaviors that are sufficiently aligned with future revisitation to serve as proxy downstream rewards. A candidate reward should (1) correlate with future revisitation rather than only immediate activity, (2) be observable within or shortly after the session, and (3) remain predictive after controlling for correlated signals. The output is a ranked list of candidates for online testing."

    {
      "source_id": "a6817bf6-d4cc-4dbd-b56e-5c02533ff462",
      "citation_number": 14,
      "cited_text": "(1) max π  𝔼 u ∼ 𝒰  [ R u | π ] . \\max_{\\pi}\\mathbb{E} {u\\sim\\mathcal{U}}[R {u}|\\pi]. 3.2. Challenges in Optimizing Long-term User Retention Optimizing long-term user retention in real-world recommender systems presents several technical challenges: • Delayed Impact and Credit Assignment: The impact of recommendations on user retention is often delayed, making it difficult to attribute the retention outcome to specific actions. For instance, a user may receive a recommendation for a new category, which sparks their interest and leads to increased engagement in the short term. However, the long-term impact of this recommendation on the user's likelihood to remain active on the platform may not be immediately observable. This temporal gap between actions and rewards complicates the credit assignment problem. • Sample Inefficiency and Exploration: The vast action space of all possible recommendations, coupled with the need for extensive exploration, can lead to sample inefficiency and suboptimal user experiences. Exploring irrelevant or poor-quality recommendations may frustrate users and hinder retention. Therefore, it is crucial to balance exploration and exploitation in a sample-efficient manner. • Complex User Preferences and Behaviors: User preferences and behaviors are dynamic and evolve over time. A user's interests may shift from one category to another over time, or their sensitivity to certain types of recommendations may change based on their current life stage or external factors. Capturing these complex dynamics and adapting to user-specific patterns is crucial for effective retention optimization."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "ab9db06f-530d-4123-adce-92e44aa285ad",
      "citation_number": 15,
      "cited_text": "Save, Revisit, Retain: A Scalable Framework for Enhancing User Retention in Large-Scale Recommender Systems Weijie Jiang, Armando Ordorica, Jaewon Yang, Olafur Gudmundsson, Yucheng Tu*, Huizhong Duan Pinterest Inc. weijiejiang,aordorica,jaewonyang,ogudmundsson,ytu,hduan@pinterest.com Abstract User retention is a critical objective for online platforms like Pinterest, as it strengthens user loyalty and drives growth through repeated engagement. A key indicator of retention is revisitation, i.e., when users return to view previously saved content, a behavior often sparked by personalized recommendations and user satisfaction. However, modeling and optimizing revisitation poses significant challenges. One core difficulty is accurate attribution: it is often unclear which specific user actions or content exposures trigger a revisit, since many confounding factors (e.g., content quality, user interface, notifications, or even changing user intent) can influence return behavior. Additionally, the scale and timing of revisitations introduce further complexity; users may revisit content days or even weeks after their initial interaction, requiring the system to maintain and associate extensive historical records across millions of users and sessions. These complexities render existing methods insufficient for robustly capturing and optimizing long-term revisitation. To address these gaps, we introduce a novel, lightweight, and interpretable framework for modeling revisitation behavior and optimizing long-term user retention in Pinterest’s search-based recommendation context. By defining a surrogate attribution process that links saves to subsequent revisitations, we reduce noise in the causal relationship between user actions and return visits. Our scalable event aggregation pipeline enables large-scale analysis of user revisitation patterns and enhances the ranking system’s ability to surface items with high retention value. Deployed on Pinterest’s Related Pins surface to serve 500+ million users, the framework led to a significant lift of 0.1% in active users without additional computational costs. Our data analysis reveals novel insights, such as the impact of content topics on revisitation rates; for example, users are more likely to revisit aesthetically pleasing topics."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "f5a62abd-107f-4425-81cc-c84115c70732",
      "citation_number": 16,
      "cited_text": "Existing Approaches At a high-level, there are two existing approaches to estimate the long-term outcome of algorithms without actually conducting long-term experiments, namely long-term causal inference (long-term CI) and typical Off-policy evaluation (typical OPE). Long-term causal inference: A conventional approach to estimating the long-term outcome of algorithmic changes is through a method known as long-term causal inference (LCI). LCI aims to achieve this by using historical data to infer the causal relationship between short-term surrogate outcomes (such as clicks, likes) and long-term outcomes (such as user retention a year from now). For this to be valid, LCI necessitates an assumption known as surrogacy. This requires that the short-term outcomes hold sufficient information to identify the distribution of the long-term outcome. However, this assumption has been considered restrictive and challenging to satisfy because it demands the presence of sufficient short-term surrogates that enable perfect identification of the long-term outcome. In other words, the connection between short-term and long-term outcomes must remain entirely consistent across all algorithms. Figure below demonstrates LCI assumption regarding surrogacy and ignoring action effects which could vary for different algorithms to estimate long-term rewards."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "1c81f0d0-b780-4875-a367-0717630d8421",
      "citation_number": 17,
      "cited_text": "Directly optimizing for long-term rewards is typically hard due to the sparsity in observing these events and the low signal-to-noise ratio (weak connection) between these long-term outcomes and a single recommendation. Therefore, researchers often leverage surrogates or mid-level outcomes that are easier to observe as a proxy for potential long-term outcomes. For example, Wang et al. ( 2022 ) investigates several surrogates such as diversity of consumption, frequency of returning to the platform, repeated consumption, etc., as a proxy to estimate long-term user engagement. The authors then use such surrogates in the objective function for the RL algorithm to optimize for those metrics. With their work, they aim at providing guidance for researchers and practitioners when selecting surrogate measures to address the difficult problem of optimizing for long-term objectives."
    },
    {
      "source_id": "7fee3f6b-6faa-436e-aa21-d9477fef7739",
      "citation_number": 18,
      "cited_text": "mization, e.g., McDonald et al. [25], Wang et al. [42], Yang et al. [44]. Several theoretical analyses derive the semiparametric efficiency bound in LCI [6, 19] and deal with unobserved confounders [40], which is of independent interest. However, all existing work in LCI relies on surrogacy or related assumptions, which are debatable and unverifiable, causing significant bias under their violations as shown in our simulations. Furthermore, the conventional LCI frame- work does not formulate an action distribution (policy) induced by"
    },

      "source_id": "2856572a-f50a-4c07-8485-d9b948ea9547",
      "citation_number": 19,
      "cited_text": "Rewards for deeper sessions +0.24% +0.48% +0.09% +0.10% Negative rewards +0.16% +0.16% +0.46% +0.24% Rewards for use case adoption +0.10% +0.10% +0.16% +0.11% Table 3: Experiment results on Homefeed based on different user states with statistically significant relative lifts. and two label definitions (duration-only vs. duration with no downstream action). The offline analysis results demonstrate that very short thresholds produce too few labels for reliable online impact, while long thresholds introduce noise and weaken revisitation prediction. Based on this analysis, we launch an initial online experiment with a uniform threshold (𝛿 = 1s). Although aggregate Homefeed metrics improve, extended measurement shows negative retention and engagement trends for non-core users due to heterogeneity of different user activities. Additional cross surface analysis shows that the treatment increases transitions from Homefeed to P2P, but also reduces engagement from other surfaces, indicating cross-surface cannibalization and a mismatch between a single threshold and heterogeneous user behavior."

      "citation_number": 20,
      "cited_text": "We then conduct an offline analysis by user state and find that the effective threshold differs across segments. This motivates a second launch with thresholds (𝛿core = 1s, 𝛿non-core = 0.5s) optimized for different user states, followed by utility weight tuning using the HyperOPT. In a month-long experiment, this configuration improves both engagement and retention-related outcomes: SS increase by +0.16%, unsuccessful sessions decrease by −0.40%, total time spent increases by +0.35%, and time spent on Homefeed and Related Pins increases by +0.2% and +0.5%, respectively.
